an = (5^{n+1}) /4 -5/4

To find the **12th** term of the given geometric progression (GP), let's follow these steps:

### Step 1: Identify the First Term and Common Ratio
- The first term (\( a \)) is \( 7 \).
- The common ratio (\( r \)) can be found by dividing the second term by the first term:
  
  \[
  r = \frac{49}{7} = 7
  \]
  
### Step 2: Use the Formula for the \( n^{th} \) Term of a GP
The general formula to find the \( n^{th} \) term of a geometric progression is:

\[
a_n = a \times r^{(n-1)}
\]

Here, we need to find the **12th** term (\( a_{12} \)).

### Step 3: Substitute the Known Values
Substitute \( a = 7 \), \( r = 7 \), and \( n = 12 \) into the formula:

\[
a_{12} = 7 \times 7^{(12-1)} = 7^{12}
\]

### Step 4: Calculate \( 7^{12} \)
Calculate \( 7^{12} \):

\[
7^{12} = 13,841,287,201
\]

### Final Answer

\[
\boxed{13,\!841,\!287,\!201}
\]

\[ a_n = ar^{n-1} \]

Plugging in the known values:
\[ 262144 = 4 \times 16^{n-1} \]

To solve for \(n\), I divide both sides by 4:
\[ 65536 = 16^{n-1} \]

Recognizing that 65536 is a power of 16, specifically \(16^4\), I set up the equation:
\[ 16^4 = 16^{n-1} \]
This implies:
\[ 4 = n - 1 \]
So,
\[ n = 5 \]

Therefore, 262144 is the 5th term of the GP.

To determine which term of the geometric progression (GP) \(4, 64, 1024, \ldots\) up to \(n\) terms is **262144**, let's follow these steps:

### Step 1: Identify the First Term and Common Ratio
- **First term (\(a\))**: The first term of the GP is \(4\).
  
  \[
  a = 4
  \]

- **Common ratio (\(r\))**: To find the common ratio, divide the second term by the first term.

  \[
  r = \frac{64}{4} = 16
  \]

### Step 2: Use the Formula for the \(n^{th}\) Term of a GP
The general formula for the \(n^{th}\) term of a geometric progression is:

\[
a_n = a \times r^{(n-1)}
\]

Given that we want to find when \(a_n = 262144\), substitute the known values into the formula:

\[
262144 = 4 \times 16^{(n-1)}
\]

### Step 3: Solve for \(n\)
1. **Divide both sides by 4** to isolate the exponential term.

   \[
   \frac{262144}{4} = 16^{(n-1)} \\
   65536 = 16^{(n-1)}
   \]

2. **Express 65536 as a power of 16** to solve for \(n\).

   Notice that:
   
   \[
   16^4 = (2^4)^4 = 2^{16} = 65536
   \]
   
   Therefore:
   
   \[
   16^4 = 16^{(n-1)}
   \]

3. **Set the exponents equal to each other** since the bases are the same.

   \[
   4 = n - 1
   \]

4. **Solve for \(n\)**:

   \[
   n = 4 + 1 \\
   n = 5
   \]

### Final Answer

The term **262144** is the \(5^{th}\) term of the geometric progression.

\[
\boxed{5}
\]

To solve the problem step-by-step:

**Given:**
- In a Geometric Progression (GP), the 3rd term (\(a_3\)) is 24.
- The 5th term (\(a_5\)) is 384.

**Find:**
- The 12th term (\(a_{12}\)) of the GP.

---

### Step 1: Understand the Formula for Geometric Progression

In a GP, each term after the first is found by multiplying the previous term by a constant called the common ratio (\(r\)). The general formula for the \(n^{th}\) term of a GP is:

\[
a_n = ar^{n-1}
\]

where:
- \(a\) is the first term,
- \(r\) is the common ratio,
- \(n\) is the term number.

---

### Step 2: Set Up Equations Using the Given Terms

Given that:
- The 3rd term (\(a_3 = 24\)):

\[
ar^{2} = 24 \quad \text{(Equation 1)}
\]

- The 5th term (\(a_5 = 384\)):

\[
ar^{4} = 384 \quad \text{(Equation 2)}
\]

---

### Step 3: Find the Common Ratio (\(r\))

Divide Equation 2 by Equation 1 to eliminate \(a\):

\[
\frac{ar^{4}}{ar^{2}} = \frac{384}{24}
\]

Simplify:

\[
r^{2} = 16
\]

Take the square root of both sides:

\[
r = \sqrt{16} \\
r = 4 \quad \text{or} \quad r = -4
\]

---

### Step 4: Find the First Term (\(a\))

**Case 1:** \(r = 4\)

Substitute \(r = 4\) into Equation 1:

\[
a(4)^2 = 24 \\
a(16) = 24 \\
a = \frac{24}{16} \\
a = 1.5
\]

**Case 2:** \(r = -4\)

Substitute \(r = -4\) into Equation 1:

\[
a(-4)^2 = 24 \\
a(16) = 24 \\
a = \frac{24}{16} \\
a = 1.5
\]

In both cases, the first term \(a\) is **1.5**.

---

### Step 5: Find the 12th Term (\(a_{12}\))

Using the general formula:

\[
a_{12} = ar^{11}
\]

Substitute \(a = 1.5\) and \(r = 4\):

\[
a_{12} = 1.5 \times (4)^{11} \\
= 1.5 \times 4,194,304 \\
= 6,291,456
\]

---

**Final Answer:**

The 12th term of the geometric progression is:

\[
\boxed{\text{Numeric answer}: 6,\!291,\!456}
\]

To determine the total number of ancestors over ten generations prior to the current generation:

1. **Identify the Pattern:** Each generation has twice as many ancestors as the previous one.

2. **Formulate the Series:** This forms a geometric series where each term is double the previous one, starting from 2 parents.

3. **Apply the Geometric Series Sum Formula:**
   
   The sum of the first n terms of a geometric series can be calculated using:
   
   \[
   S_n = a_1 \times \frac{r^n - 1}{r - 1}
   \]
   
   Where \(a_1 = 2\) and \(r = 2\).

4. **Calculate the Sum:**

   \[
   S_{10} = 2 \times \frac{2^{10} - 1}{2 - 1} = 2 \times (1023) = 2046
   \]

To determine the total number of ancestors a person has over ten generations preceding their own, we can analyze the pattern of inheritance:

1. **Generation Breakdown:**
   
   - **1st Generation (Parents):** 2
   - **2nd Generation (Grandparents):** 4
   - **3rd Generation (Great-grandparents):** 8
   - ...
   - **n-th Generation:** \(2^n\)

2. **Summing the Series:**
   
   We need to find the sum of the first ten terms of this geometric series where each term doubles:
   
   \[
   S = 2 + 4 + 8 + \ldots + 2^{10}
   \]
   
   The formula for the sum of a geometric series is:
   
   \[
   S_n = a_1 \times \frac{r^n - 1}{r - 1}
   \]
   
   Where:
   - \(a_1 = 2\) (first term)
   - \(r = 2\) (common ratio)
   - \(n = 10\) (number of terms)

3. **Calculating the Sum:**
   
   Plugging in the values:
   
   \[

To solve this problem, we need to insert three numbers between \(1\) and \(4096\) such that the resulting sequence forms a **Geometric Progression (GP)**.

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Understand the Problem:**
   - We have two known terms in the GP:
     - First term (\(a_1\)) = 1
     - Fifth term (\(a_5\)) = 4096
   - We need to find three numbers that lie between these two terms to complete the GP.

2. **Recall the Formula for the \(n^{th}\) Term of a GP:**
   \[
   a_n = a_1 \times r^{(n-1)}
   \]
   where:
   - \(a_n\) is the \(n^{th}\) term
   - \(r\) is the common ratio

3. **Find the Common Ratio (\(r\)):**
   Using the fifth term:
   \[
   a_5 = 1 \times r^{4} = 4096
   \]
   Solving for \(r\):
   \[
   r^4 = 4096 \\
   r = \sqrt[4]{4096} \\
   r = 8
   \]

     \]

     \[

     \]

     \]

To find the 14th term of a geometric progression, I first need to recall the formula for the nth term of such a sequence.

The general formula for the nth term is:

a_n = a_1 * r^(n-1)

where:
- a_n represents the nth term,
- a_1 is the first term,
- r is the common ratio,
- n is the term number.

I know that the 10th term (a_10) is 384, and the common ratio (r) is 4. I can use these values to find the first term (a_1).

Starting with the formula:

a_10 = a_1 * r^(10-1)
384 = a_1 * 4^9

Calculating 4 raised to the 9th power gives me 262,144. So,

384 = a_1 * 262,144
a_1 = 384 / 262,144
a_1 ≈ 0.00146

Now that I have the first term, I can find the 14th term (a_14) using the same formula:

a_14 = a_1 * r^(14-1)
a_14 = 0.00146 * 4^13
a_14 ≈ 0.00146 * 67,108,864
a_14 ≈ 97,502

Therefore, the 14th term of the geometric progression is approximately 97,502.
</think>

To find the **14th term** of a geometric progression where the **10th term** is 384 and the **common ratio (r)** is 4, follow these steps:

### Step 1: Understand the Formula
The general formula for the \(n^{th}\) term (\(a_n\)) of a geometric sequence is:
\[ a_n = a_1 \times r^{(n-1)} \]
where:
- \(a_1\) is the first term,
- \(r\) is the common ratio.

### Step 2: Find the First Term (\(a_1\))
Given that the 10th term (\(a_{10}\)) is 384 and \(r = 4\), plug these values into the formula:
\[ a_{10} = a_1 \times r^{9} \]
\[ 384 = a_1 \times 4^{9} \]

Calculate \(4^9\):
\[ 4^9 = 262,144 \]

Now solve for \(a_1\):
\[ a_1 = \frac{384}{262,144} \]
\[ a_1 \approx 0.00146 \]

### Step 3: Find the 14th Term (\(a_{14}\))
Now that you have \(a_1\), use it to find \(a_{14}\):
\[ a_{14} = a_1 \times r^{13} \]
\[ a_{14} = 0.00146 \times 4^{13} \]

Calculate \(4^{13}\):
\[ 4^{13} = 67,108,864 \]

Now multiply:
\[ a_{14} \approx 0.00146 \times 67,108,864 \]
\[ a_{14} \approx 97,502 \]

### Final Answer
\[
\boxed{97502}
\]

Step-by-Step Explanation:

1. **Identify the Initial Number of Bacteria (B₀):**
   - The initial count of bacteria in the culture is given as **50**.

2. **Determine the Growth Factor:**
   - The bacteria triple every hour, meaning the growth factor (**g**) is **3**.

3. **Define the Exponential Growth Formula:**
   - The general formula for exponential growth is:
     \[
     B(t) = B₀ \times g^t
     \]
     where:
     - \( B(t) \) is the number of bacteria at time \( t \).
     - \( B₀ \) is the initial number of bacteria.
     - \( g \) is the growth factor.
     - \( t \) is the time in hours.

4. **Substitute the Known Values into the Formula:**
   - For this problem, we want to find the number of bacteria after **10 hours**:
     \[
     B(10) = 50 \times 3^{10}
     \]

5. **Calculate \( 3^{10} \):**
   - Compute \( 3^{10} \):
     \[
     3^{10} = 59,049
     \]

6. **Multiply by the Initial Number of Bacteria:**
   - Now, multiply this result by the initial count:
     \[
     B(10) = 50 \times 59,049 = 2,952,450
     \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{2,\!952,\!450}

To solve the problem of finding the amount after a certain number of years with simple interest, we can use the formula:

\[ A = P \times (1 + r \times t) \]

Where:
- \( A \) is the amount
- \( P \) is the principal amount (Rs. 10,000)
- \( r \) is the annual interest rate (5% or 0.05)
- \( t \) is the time in years

### Step-by-Step Calculations:

#### **Amount after 15th Year:**
1. **Plug in the values** for \( P = 10,000 \), \( r = 0.05 \), and \( t = 15 \):
   \[ A = 10,000 \times (1 + 0.05 \times 15) \]
2. **Calculate the interest component**:
   \[ 0.05 \times 15 = 0.75 \]
3. **Add to 1 and multiply by principal**:
   \[ A = 10,000 \times (1 + 0.75) = 10,000 \times 1.75 = 17,500 \]

#### **Total Amount after 20 Years:**
1. **Use the same formula** with \( t = 20 \):
   \[ A = 10,000 \times (1 + 0.05 \times 20) \]
2. **Calculate the interest component**:
   \[ 0.05 \times 20 = 1.00 \]
3. **Add to 1 and multiply by principal**:
   \[ A = 10,000 \times (1 + 1) = 10,000 \times 2 = 20,000 \]

### Final Answers:

- **Amount after 15th year:** Rs. 17,500
- **Total amount after 20 years:** Rs. 20,000

- \( t \) is the time in years.

\[ \text{Interest}_n = 2000 \times 0.16 \times n \]

\[ \text{Interest}_n = 320n \]

- Principal (\( P \)) = ₹2000
- Annual simple interest rate (\( r \)) = 16% or 0.16 per annum

**Step 1: Understanding Simple Interest**
Simple interest is calculated using the formula:
\[ I = P \times r \times t \]
where:
- \( I \) is the interest,
- \( P \) is the principal amount,
- \( r \) is the annual interest rate (in decimal),
- \( t \) is the time in years.

**Step 2: Calculating Interest for Each Year**
Since the investment period is yearly, the time (\( t \)) increases by 1 each year. Therefore, the interest at the end of each year forms a sequence:
\[ I_1 = P \times r \times 1 \]
\[ I_2 = P \times r \times 2 \]
\[ I_3 = P \times r \times 3 \]
\[ \vdots \]
\[ I_n = P \times r \times n \]

Substituting the given values:
\[ I_n = 2000 \times 0.16 \times n \]
\[ I_n = 320n \]

**Step 3: Identifying if It's an Arithmetic Progression (AP)**
The sequence of interests is:
\[ 320, 640, 960, \ldots \]
Here, the difference between consecutive terms is constant (\( d = 320 \)). Hence, it forms an AP with:
- First term (\( a \)) = ₹320
- Common difference (\( d \)) = ₹320

**Step 4: Finding the Total Interest for 30 Years Using AP Sum Formula**
The sum of the first \( n \) terms of an AP is given by:
\[ S_n = \frac{n}{2} \times [2a + (n - 1)d] \]

Substituting \( n = 30 \), \( a = 320 \), and \( d = 320 \):
\[

To solve for the **58th** term of the given Arithmetic Progression (AP), let's follow these steps:

### Given:
- **Total number of terms (n):** 100
- **6th term (a₆):** 24
- **Last term (a₁₀₀):** 212

### Step 1: Understanding the AP Formula
The general formula for the \( n^{th} \) term of an AP is:
\[ a_n = a + (n - 1)d \]
where:
- \( a \) is the first term,
- \( d \) is the common difference.

### Step 2: Setting Up Equations
Using the given information:

1. **For the 6th term (\( n = 6 \)):**
\[ a_6 = a + (6 - 1)d = 24 \]
\[ a + 5d = 24 \quad \text{(Equation 1)} \]

2. **For the last term (\( n = 100 \)):**
\[ a_{100} = a + (100 - 1)d = 212 \]
\[ a + 99d = 212 \quad \text{(Equation 2)} \]

### Step 3: Solving the System of Equations
Subtract **Equation 1** from **Equation 2** to eliminate \( a \):
\[
(a + 99d) - (a + 5d) = 212 - 24 \\
94d = 188 \\
d = \frac{188}{94} \\
d = 2
\]

Now, substitute \( d = 2 \) back into **Equation 1** to find \( a \):
\[
a + 5(2) = 24 \\
a + 10 = 24 \\
a = 24 - 10 \\
a = 14
\]

### Step 4: Finding the 58th Term (\( a_{58} \))
Using the AP formula for \( n = 58 \):
\[
a_{58} = a + (58 - 1)d \\
a_{58} = 14 + 57 \times 2 \\
a_{58} = 14 + 114 \\
a_{58} = 128
\]

### Final Answer:
\[
\boxed{128}
\]

- Starting year (n₁) = 1995
- Initial annual salary (a) = ₹10,000
- Annual increment (d) = ₹400

We need to determine in which year Subba Rao's income reached ₹14,000.

The salary each year forms an arithmetic sequence where:
- The first term (a₁) is the initial salary: a₁ = 10,000
- The common difference (d) is the annual increment: d = 400

The nth term of an arithmetic sequence can be found using the formula:
aₙ = a + (n - 1)d

We need to find the year (n) when the salary (aₙ) reaches 14,000:
14,000 = 10,000 + (n - 1) * 400

First, subtract 10,000 from both sides to isolate the term with n:
14,000 - 10,000 = (n - 1) * 400
4,000 = (n - 1) * 400

Next, divide both sides by 400 to solve for (n - 1):
4,000 / 400 = n - 1
10 = n - 1

Finally, add 1 to both sides to find the value of n:

To determine the year Subba Rao's income reached ₹14,000, we'll analyze the arithmetic progression of his salary over the years.

**Given:**
- **Starting Year (n₁):** 1995
- **Initial Salary (a):** ₹10,000
- **Annual Increment (d):** ₹400

We need to find the year (n) when his salary reaches ₹14,000.

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Understand the Arithmetic Sequence:**
   - The salary each year forms an arithmetic sequence where:
     - \( a = 10,000 \)
     - \( d = 400 \)
   
2. **Find the nth Term Formula:**
   - The general formula for the nth term of an arithmetic sequence is:
     \[
     a_n = a + (n - 1)d
     \]
   
3. **Set Up the Equation to Find When Salary Reaches ₹14,000:**
   \[
   14,000 = 10,000 + (n - 1) \times 400
   \]
   
4. **Solve for \( n \):**
   \[
   14,000 - 10,000 = (n - 1) \times 400 \\
   4,000 = (n - 1) \times 400 \\
   \frac{4,000}{400} = n - 1 \\
   10 = n - 1 \\
   n = 11
   \]
   
5. **Determine the Year:**
   - Since \( n = 11 \) corresponds to the 11th year after starting in 1995:
     \[
     1995 + (11 - 1) = 2005
     \]
   
**Final Answer:**

\boxed{2005}

\[ a_n = 24 + (n-1)(-3) = 27 - n \]

\[ S_n = \frac{n}{2} [2a + (n-1)d] \]

Certainly! Let's solve the problem step by step.

**Problem:**
Determine how many terms must be taken from the Arithmetic Progression (AP): 24, 21, 18, … so that their sum is 78.

**Solution:**

1. **Identify the Given Values:**
   
   - First term (\( a \)) = 24
   - Common difference (\( d \)) = 21 - 24 = -3
   - Sum of \( n \) terms (\( S_n \)) = 78

2. **Use the Formula for the Sum of an AP:**
   
   The sum of the first \( n \) terms of an AP is given by:
   \[
   S_n = \frac{n}{2} [2a + (n - 1)d]
   \]

3. **Plug in the Known Values and Set Up the Equation:**
   
   Substituting the values into the formula:
   \[
   78 = \frac{n}{2} [2(24) + (n - 1)(-3)]
   \]
   Simplify inside the brackets:
   \[
   78 = \frac{n}{2} [48 - 3(n - 1)]
   \]
   \[
   78 = \frac{n}{2} [51 - 3n]
   \]

4. **Multiply Both Sides by 2 to Eliminate the Fraction:**
   
   \[
   156 = n(51 - 3n)
   \]
   Expand the equation:
   \[
   156 = 51n - 3n^2
   \]

5. **Rearrange the Equation to Standard Quadratic Form:**
   
   Bring all terms to one side:
   \[
   3n^2 - 51n + 156 = 0
   \]
   Divide the entire equation by 3 to simplify:
   \[
   n^2 - 17n + 52 = 0
   \]

6. **Solve the Quadratic Equation:**
   
   We can solve for \( n \) using the quadratic formula:
   \[
   n = \frac{17 \pm \sqrt{(-17)^2 - 4(1)(52)}}{2(1)}
   \]
   Calculate the discriminant:
   \[
   \Delta = 289 - 208 = 81
   \]
   Take the square root of the discriminant:
   \[
   \sqrt{81} = 9
   \]
   Now, find \( n \):
   \[
   n = \frac{17 \pm 9}{2}
   \]
   
   This gives two solutions:
   \[
   n = \frac{17 + 9}{2} = \frac{26}{2} = 13
   \]
   and
   \[
   n = \frac{17 - 9}{2} = \frac{8}{2} = 4
   \]

7. **Determine the Valid Solution:**
   
   Since \( n \) represents the number of terms, it must be a positive integer. Both solutions (\( n = 13 \) and \( n = 4 \)) are valid because:
   - For \( n = 4 \): The sum is 78.
   - For \( n = 13 \): The sum also reaches 78 at the 13th term.

**Final Answer:**
The number of terms required can be either **4** or **13** to achieve a sum of 78 in the given AP. However, typically, we consider the smallest positive integer solution unless specified otherwise. Therefore, the primary answer is:

\[
\boxed{4}
\]

To find the **common difference** of an Arithmetic Progression (AP) where the 34th term exceeds its 20th term by **14**, follow these steps:

1. **Recall the formula for the nth term of an AP:**
   
   \[
   a_n = a_1 + (n - 1)d
   \]
   
   - \(a_n\) is the nth term,
   - \(a_1\) is the first term,
   - \(d\) is the common difference.

2. **Express the 34th and 20th terms using the formula:**
   
   \[
   a_{34} = a_1 + (34 - 1)d = a_1 + 33d
   \]
   
   \[
   a_{20} = a_1 + (20 - 1)d = a_1 + 19d
   \]

3. **Set up the equation based on the given difference:**
   
   According to the problem, \(a_{34}\) exceeds \(a_{20}\) by 14:
   
   \[
   a_{34} - a_{20} = 14
   \]
   
   Substitute the expressions from step 2 into this equation:
   
   \[
   (a_1 + 33d) - (a_1 + 19d) = 14
   \]

4. **Simplify and solve for \(d\):**
   
   \[
   a_1 + 33d - a_1 - 19d = 14 \\
   14d = 14 \\
   d = \frac{14}{14} \\
   d = 1
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{1}
\]

Given the problem of determining if there exist numbers that satisfy both conditions: their sum is 12 times their geometric mean, and they are in the ratio \(6 + \sqrt{35}\) to \(6 - \sqrt{35}\).

First, we note that for two numbers \(a\) and \(b\), if they are in the ratio \(k : m\), then we can express them as \(a = kt\) and \(b = mt\) for some positive real number \(t\). The condition given is that their sum equals 12 times their geometric mean.

We start by expressing the numbers in terms of a common variable:
- Let \(a = (6 + \sqrt{35})t\)
- Let \(b = (6 - \sqrt{35})t\)

The sum of these numbers is:
\[ a + b = (6 + \sqrt{35})t + (6 - \sqrt{35})t = 12t \]

The geometric mean of these numbers is:
\[ \sqrt{ab} = \sqrt{(6 + \sqrt{35})t \cdot (6 - \sqrt{35})t} = \sqrt{(36 - 35)t^2} = \sqrt{t^2} = t \]

According to the given condition, the sum should be 12 times the geometric mean:
\[ a + b = 12 \cdot \text{geometric mean} \]
\[ 12t = 12t \]

This equation holds true for any positive real number \(t\). Therefore, such numbers exist and are of the form \((6 + \sqrt{35}, 6 - \sqrt{35})\) scaled by a positive constant.

To calculate the **geometric mean** of the annual percentage growth rates of profits from the year 2000 to 2005, follow these steps:

### Step 1: List the Data
The given data points are:
\[ 50\%,\ 72\%,\ 54\%,\ 82\%,\ 93\% \]

### Step 2: Convert Percentages to Decimal Form
Convert each percentage into its decimal form by dividing by \(100\):
\[
\frac{50}{100} = 0.5,\quad
\frac{72}{100} = 0.72,\quad
\frac{54}{100} = 0.54,\quad
\frac{82}{100} = 0.82,\quad
\frac{93}{100} = 0.93
\]

\[
0.5 \times 0.72 \times 0.54 \times 0.82 \times 0.93
\]

\[
0.5 \times 0.72 = 0.36
\]
\[
0.36 \times 0.54 = 0.1944
\]
\[
0.1944 \times 0.82 = 0.1592
\]
\[
0.1592 \times 0.93 = 0.147
\]

### Step 4: Take the Nth Root of the Product
Since there are \(5\) data points, we take the fifth root:
\[
\sqrt[5]{0.147} \approx 0.658
\]
Converting back to percentage form by multiplying by \(100\):
\[
0.658 \times 100 = 65.8\%

**Final Answer**
The growth rate \( r \) can be found using the formula \( r = \sqrt[3]{\frac{A_1 + A_2 + A_3}{B_1 + B_2 + B_3}} - 1 \). Thus, the solution is \boxed{\sqrt[3]{\frac{A_1 + A_2 + A_3}{B_1 + B_2 + B_3}} - 1}.
</think>

The user provided a formula for calculating the growth rate \( r \) over three periods. The formula involves taking the cube root of the ratio of the sums of two sets of values and then subtracting 1. This is essentially the Compound Annual Growth Rate (CAGR).

Given the initial value or investment that grows over three periods, the CAGR is calculated by taking the cube root of the total growth factor and subtracting 1. The formula for this is:

\[ r = \sqrt[3]{\frac{A_1 + A_2 + A_3}{B_1 + B_2 + B_3}} - 1 \]

Thus, the solution is:

\[
\boxed{\sqrt[3]{\frac{A_1 + A_2 + A_3}{B_1 + B_2 + B_3}} - 1}
\]

To determine the value of \( k \) in the given arithmetic sequence where the arithmetic mean of the numbers \( 9, 8, 10, k, 12 \) is 15, follow these steps:

**Step 1: Understand the Formula for Arithmetic Mean**

The arithmetic mean (\( \text{AM} \)) of a set of numbers is calculated by summing all the numbers and dividing by the count of numbers.

\[
\text{AM} = \frac{\text{Sum of all terms}}{\text{Number of terms}}
\]

**Step 2: Set Up the Equation**

Given:
- The arithmetic mean (\( \text{AM} \)) is \( 15 \).
- The numbers are \( 9, 8, 10, k, 12 \).

There are \( 5 \) terms in total.

\[
\frac{9 + 8 + 10 + k + 12}{5} = 15
\]

**Step 3: Simplify the Equation**

First, sum the known numbers:

\[
9 + 8 + 10 + 12 = 39
\]

Now, substitute back into the equation:

\[
\frac{39 + k}{5} = 15
\]

**Step 4: Solve for \( k \)**

Multiply both sides of the equation by \( 5 \) to eliminate the denominator:

\[
39 + k = 75
\]

Subtract \( 39 \) from both sides to isolate \( k \):

\[
k = 75 - 39
\]
\[
k = 36
\]

**Final Answer:**

The value of \( k \) is:

\boxed{36}

**Solution:**

We are given:
- The mean of 14 numbers is **6**.

To find:
- The new mean after adding **3** to each of the numbers.

---

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Find the Total Sum of the Original Numbers**
   
   \[
   \text{Mean} = \frac{\text{Total Sum}}{\text{Number of Terms}}
   \]
   
   Rearranging the formula:
   
   \[
   \text{Total Sum} = \text{Mean} \times \text{Number of Terms}
   \]
   
   Plugging in the given values:
   
   \[
   \text{Total Sum} = 6 \times 14 = 84
   \]

2. **Calculate the New Total Sum After Adding 3 to Each Number**
   
   Since we are adding **3** to each of the **14 numbers**, the total increase in sum is:
   
   \[
   \text{Increase} = 3 \times 14 = 42
   \]
   
   Therefore, the new total sum is:
   
   \[
   \text{New Total Sum} = 84 + 42 = 126
   \]

3. **Find the New Mean**
   
   The number of terms remains the same (14).
   
   Using the mean formula again:
   
   \[
   \text{New Mean} = \frac{\text{New Total Sum}}{\text{Number of Terms}} = \frac{126}{14} = 9
   \]

---

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{9}

To determine the correct mean of the numbers after accounting for the misinterpretation, follow these steps:

1. **Calculate the Initial Total Sum**:
   
   The arithmetic mean of the 40 numbers is given as 38. Therefore, the total sum \( S \) can be calculated as:
   \[
   S = \text{Mean} \times \text{Number of terms} = 38 \times 40 = 1520
   \]

2. **Adjust for the Misinterpretation**:
   
   It was discovered that one number was incorrectly recorded as 36 instead of its true value, which is 56. The difference between the correct and incorrect values is:
   \[
   \text{Difference} = 56 - 36 = 20
   \]
   
   To find the correct total sum \( S' \), add this difference to the initial sum:
   \[
   S' = S + \text{Difference} = 1520 + 20 = 1540
   \]

3. **Calculate the Correct Mean**:
   
   Now, compute the correct mean using the adjusted total sum and the number of terms (which remains 40):
   \[
   \text{Correct Mean} = \frac{S'}{\text{Number of terms}} = \frac{1540}{40} = 38.5
   \]

### Step 1: Identify the First Term and Common Ratio
The first term (\(a_1\)) of the GP is:
\[ a_1 = 7 \]

The common ratio (\(r\)) can be found by dividing the second term by the first term:
\[ r = \frac{49}{7} = 7 \]

### Step 2: Use the Formula for the \(n^{th}\) Term of a GP
The formula to find the \(n^{th}\) term (\(a_n\)) of a GP is:
\[ a_n = a_1 \times r^{(n-1)} \]

\[ a_{12} = 7 \times 7^{11} \]

To determine which term of the geometric progression (GP) \(4, 64, 1024, \ldots\) corresponds to the value \(262144\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Identify the First Term and Common Ratio
- **First term (\(a\))**: The first term of the GP is given as:
  \[

  \]
  
- **Common Ratio (\(r\))**: Calculate the common ratio by dividing the second term by the first term:
  \[
  r = \frac{64}{4} = 16
  \]

### Step 2: Use the General Formula for the \(n^{th}\) Term of a GP
The general formula for the \(n^{th}\) term (\(T_n\)) of a geometric progression is:
\[
T_n = a \times r^{(n-1)}
\]
Given that we want to find when \(T_n = 262144\), set up the equation:
\[
262144 = 4 \times 16^{(n-1)}
\]

\]

\[

\]

The term \(262144\) is the **fifth** term of the geometric progression.

The sum of the first \( n \) terms and specifically the first 5 terms of the geometric series is calculated using the formula for the sum of a geometric series.

**Sum of the first \( n \) terms:**

\[ S_n = a \frac{1 - r^n}{1 - r} \]

Where:
- First term (\( a \)) = 3
- Common ratio (\( r \)) = \( \frac{2}{3} \)

Plugging in the values:

\[ S_n = 3 \times \frac{1 - \left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^n}{1 - \frac{2}{3}} \]
\[ S_n = 3 \times \frac{1 - \left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^n}{\frac{1}{3}} \]
\[ S_n = 9 \times \left(1 - \left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^n\right) \]

**Sum of the first 5 terms:**

Calculate each term and sum them up:

- Term 1: \( 3 \)
- Term 2: \( 6/3 = 2 \)
- Term 3: \( 12/9 = 4/3 \approx 1.333... \)
- Term 4: \( 24/27 = 8/9 \approx 0.888... \)
- Term 5: \( 48/81 = 16/27 \approx 0.592... \)

Adding these together:

\[ S_5 = 3 + 2 + \frac{4}{3} + \frac{8}{9} + \frac{16}{27} \]
Convert all to denominator 27:
- \( 3 = \frac{81}{27} \)
- \( 2 = \frac{54}{27} \)
- \( \frac{4}{3} = \frac{36}{27} \)
- \( \frac{8}{9} = \frac{24}{27} \)
- \( \frac{16}{27} \) remains

Adding them up:

\[ S_5 = \frac{81 + 54 + 36 + 24 + 16}{27} = \frac{211}{27} \]

So, the final answers are:

Sum of first \( n \) terms: 
\[
\boxed{S_n = 9 \left(1 - \left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^n\right)}
\]

Sum of first 5 terms:

The problem involves finding the common ratio of a geometric progression where the sum of the first three terms is 13/12 and their product is -1. 

Let the three terms be \(a\), \(ar\), and \(ar^2\). The sum is:
\[ a + ar + ar^2 = \frac{13}{12} \]
The product is:
\[ a \cdot ar \cdot ar^2 = (ar)^3 = -1 \]
Solving for \(ar\) gives \(ar = -1\), so \(a = -\frac{1}{r}\). Substituting into the sum equation leads to solving the quadratic equation, resulting in two possible values for \(r\): \(-\frac{3}{4}\) and \(-\frac{4}{3}\).

**Answer:** The common ratios are \(\boxed{-\dfrac{3}{4}}\) and \(\boxed{-\dfrac{4}{3}}\).

The number of ancestors forms a geometric sequence where each term doubles the previous one: 2, 4, 8, ..., up to the 10th generation. The sum of this series gives the total number of ancestors.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Sequence Type:** It's a geometric progression with first term \( a = 2 \) and common ratio \( r = 2 \).

2. **Determine the Number of Terms (n):** We're considering ten generations preceding, so n = 10.

3. **Use the Geometric Series Sum Formula:**
   
   The sum \( S_n \) is given by:
   \[
   S_n = a \times \frac{r^n - 1}{r - 1}
   \]
   
4. **Plug in the Values:**
   
   Substituting \( a = 2 \), \( r = 2 \), and \( n = 10 \):
   \[
   S_{10} = 2 \times \frac{2^{10} - 1}{2 - 1} = 2 \times (1024 - 1) = 2 \times 1023 = 2046
   \]

**Final Answer:**
The total number of ancestors during the ten generations preceding his own is 2046.

To determine the 14th term of the geometric progression, I start by recalling that each term can be found using the formula \( a_n = ar^{n-1} \), where \( a \) is the first term and \( r \) is the common ratio.

Given that the 10th term (\( a_{10} \)) is 384 and the common ratio (\( r \)) is 4, I can set up the equation:
\[ ar^{9} = 384 \]

Substituting \( r = 4 \) into the equation gives:
\[ a \times 4^9 = 384 \]
Calculating \( 4^9 \), which equals 262,144, so:
\[ a \times 262,144 = 384 \]

Solving for \( a \) involves dividing both sides by 262,144:
\[ a = \frac{384}{262,144} \]
This simplifies to:
\[ a = \frac{3}{2048} \]

Now that I have the first term \( a = \frac{3}{2048} \), I can find the 14th term (\( a_{14} \)) using the same formula:
\[ a_{14} = ar^{13} \]
Substituting the known values:
\[ a_{14} = \left(\frac{3}{2048}\right) \times 4^{13} \]

Calculating \( 4^{13} \), which equals 67,108,864, so:
\[ a_{14} = \frac{3}{2048} \times 67,108,864 \]

\[ a_{14} = 9,953,28 \]

To find the **14th** term of the Geometric Progression (GP) given that the **10th** term is 384 and the common ratio (\( r \)) is 4, follow these steps:

### Step 1: Understand the Formula for the \( n^{th} \) Term of a GP

The general formula for the \( n^{th} \) term of a geometric progression is:
\[
a_n = a_1 \times r^{(n-1)}
\]
where:
- \( a_n \) is the \( n^{th} \) term,
- \( a_1 \) is the first term,
- \( r \) is the common ratio,
- \( n \) is the term number.

### Step 2: Use the Given Information to Find the First Term (\( a_1 \))

Given:
- The **10th** term (\( a_{10} \)) = 384
- Common ratio (\( r \)) = 4

Using the formula for \( a_{10} \):
\[
a_{10} = a_1 \times r^{9}
\]
Substitute the known values:
\[
384 = a_1 \times 4^{9}
\]
Calculate \( 4^9 \):
\[
4^9 = 262,\!144
\]
So,
\[
a_1 = \frac{384}{262,\!144} = \frac{3}{2048}
\]

### Step 3: Find the 14th Term (\( a_{14} \))

Now, use the formula to find \( a_{14} \):
\[
a_{14} = a_1 \times r^{13}
\]
Substitute the known values:
\[
a_{14} = \left(\frac{3}{2048}\right) \times 4^{13}
\]
Calculate \( 4^{13} \):
\[
4^{13} = 67,\!108,\!864
\]
So,
\[
a_{14} = \frac{3}{2048} \times 67,\!108,\!864 = 9,\!953,\!28
\]

### Final Answer

The **14th** term of the Geometric Progression is:
\[
\boxed{9,\!953,\!28}
\]

First, I'll identify the terms involved in a geometric progression (GP). The general form of a GP is \( a_n = ar^{n-1} \), where \( a \) is the first term and \( r \) is the common ratio.

Given that the sixth term (\( a_6 \)) is the square of the fourth term (\( a_4^2 \)), I can set up the equation:
\[
ar^5 = (ar^3)^2
\]
Simplifying this, we get:
\[
r^5 = r^6
\]
Dividing both sides by \( r^5 \) leads to:
\[
1 = r
\]
So, the common ratio \( r \) is 1.

With \( a = -10 \) and \( r = 1 \), I can find the ninth term (\( a_9 \)):
\[
a_9 = ar^8 = (-10)(1)^8 = -10
\]
</think>

**Solution:**

We are given that the sixth term of a geometric progression (GP) is the square of its fourth term, and the first term \( a \) is \(-10\). We need to find the ninth term.

1. **Understanding the GP Terms:**
   
   In a GP with first term \( a \) and common ratio \( r \), the \( n^{th} \) term is given by:
   \[
   T_n = ar^{n-1}
   \]
   
2. **Setting Up the Given Condition:**
   
   - The fourth term (\( T_4 \)) is:
     \[
     T_4 = ar^{3}
     \]
     
   - The sixth term (\( T_6 \)) is given to be the square of \( T_4 \):
     \[
     T_6 = (T_4)^2
     \]
     
   Substituting \( T_4 \) into the equation:
   \[
   ar^{5} = (ar^{3})^2
   \]
   
3. **Simplifying the Equation:**
   
   \[
   ar^{5} = a^2r^{6}
   \]
   
   Divide both sides by \( ar^{5} \):
   \[
   1 = ar
   \]
   
   Therefore:
   \[
   r = 0 \quad \text{or} \quad a = \frac{1}{r}
   \]
   
   However, since the first term \( a = -10 \), we have:
   \[
   r = \frac{1}{-10} = -\frac{1}{10}
   \]
   
4. **Finding the Ninth Term (\( T_9 \)):**
   
   Using the GP formula for the ninth term:
   \[
   T_9 = ar^{8} = (-10) \left(-\frac{1}{10}\right)^{8}
   \]
   
   Simplifying:
   \[
   T_9 = -10 \times \left(\frac{1}{10^8}\right) = -\frac{10}{10^8} = -\frac{1}{10^7}
   \]
   
5. **Final Answer:**

   The ninth term of the geometric progression is:
   \[
   \boxed{-\dfrac{1}{10^{7}}}
   \]

= a(1 + r)(1 - r^{2n}) / (1 - r²)

Given that \( SYS < R < SHT \), we need to find the common ratio \( r \) such that the sum of all terms in a geometric progression (GP) with an even number of terms is 5 times the sum of the terms at odd positions.

1. Let the first term be \( a \) and the common ratio be \( r \). The GP has \( 2n \) terms.
2. The total sum of the GP is given by:
   \[
   S_{\text{total}} = a \frac{1 - r^{2n}}{1 - r}
   \]
3. The sum of the terms at odd positions (which are also \( n \) terms) is given by:
   \[
   S_{\text{odd}} = a + ar^2 + ar^4 + \cdots + ar^{2(n-1)} = a \frac{1 - r^{2n}}{1 - r^2}
   \]
4. According to the problem, we have:
   \[
   S_{\text{total}} = 5 \cdot S_{\text{odd}}
   \]
5. Substituting the expressions for \( S_{\text{total}} \) and \( S_{\text{odd}} \):
   \[
   a \frac{1 - r^{2n}}{1 - r} = 5 \cdot a \frac{1 - r^{2n}}{1 - r^2}
   \]
6. Canceling \( a \) and \( 1 - r^{2n} \) from both sides (assuming they are non-zero):
   \[
   \frac{1}{1 - r} = \frac{5}{1 - r^2}
   \]
7. Simplifying the equation:
   \[
   1 - r^2 = 5(1 - r)
   \]
8. Expanding and rearranging terms:
   \[
   1 - r^2 = 5 - 5r
   \]
   \[
   -r^2 + 5r - 4 = 0
   \]
9. Solving the quadratic equation \( r^2 - 5r + 4 = 0 \):
   \[
   (r - 1)(r - 4) = 0
   \]
   Thus, \( r = 1 \) or \( r = 4 \).

10. Checking if \( r = 1 \) is a valid solution:
    - If \( r = 1 \), the sum of terms at odd positions would be equal to the total sum, which contradicts the given condition.
    - Therefore, \( r = 1 \) is not a valid solution.

Thus, the common ratio is \(\boxed{4}\).

The third term (\( n = 3 \)) is:
\[ a_3 = 10r^2 \]

The fifth term (\( n = 5 \)) is:
\[ a_5 = 10r^4 \]

According to the problem, their sum is 900. So I set up the equation:
\[ 10r^2 + 10r^4 = 900 \]

To simplify, I divide both sides by 10:
\[ r^2 + r^4 = 90 \]
Which can be rewritten as:
\[ r^4 + r^2 - 90 = 0 \]

This is a quadratic equation in terms of \( y = r^2 \). Let me substitute to make it clearer:
\[ y^2 + y - 90 = 0 \]

Solving this using the quadratic formula:
\[ y = \frac{-b \pm \sqrt{b^2 - 4ac}}{2a} \]
Where \( a = 1 \), \( b = 1 \), and \( c = -90 \):
\[ y = \frac{-1 \pm \sqrt{1 + 360}}{2} \]
\[ y = \frac{-1 \pm \sqrt{361}}{2} \]
\[ y = \frac{-1 \pm 19}{2} \]

This gives two solutions:
\[ y = 9 \quad \text{and} \quad y = -10 \]

Since \( y = r^2 \), it must be non-negative, so we discard \( y = -10 \). Thus:
\[ r^2 = 9 \]
Taking the square root of both sides gives two possible values for \( r \):
\[ r = 3 \quad \text{or} \quad r = -3 \]

Both solutions are valid in a GP context.
</think>

To solve the problem, let's analyze it step by step.

**Given:**
- First term (\(a\)) of the Geometric Progression (GP) is \(10\).
- The sum of the third term and the fifth term is \(900\).

**Solution:**

In a GP, each term can be expressed as:
\[ a_n = ar^{n-1} \]
where:
- \(a\) is the first term,
- \(r\) is the common ratio,
- \(n\) is the term number.

Given that the first term (\(a\)) is \(10\), we have:

1. **Third Term (\(a_3\)):**
   \[ a_3 = ar^{2} = 10r^{2} \]

2. **Fifth Term (\(a_5\)):**
   \[ a_5 = ar^{4} = 10r^{4} \]

According to the problem, their sum is \(900\):
\[ a_3 + a_5 = 900 \]
Substituting the expressions for \(a_3\) and \(a_5\):
\[ 10r^{2} + 10r^{4} = 900 \]

Divide both sides by \(10\) to simplify:
\[ r^{2} + r^{4} = 90 \]
Rearrange the equation:
\[ r^{4} + r^{2} - 90 = 0 \]

Let's let \( y = r^2 \), so the equation becomes a quadratic in terms of \(y\):
\[ y^{2} + y - 90 = 0 \]

Solve this quadratic equation using the quadratic formula:
\[ y = \frac{-b \pm \sqrt{b^2 - 4ac}}{2a} \]
where \( a = 1 \), \( b = 1 \), and \( c = -90 \):
\[
y = \frac{-1 \pm \sqrt{(1)^2 - 4(1)(-90)}}{2(1)} \\
y = \frac{-1 \pm \sqrt{1 + 360}}{2} \\
y = \frac{-1 \pm \sqrt{361}}{2} \\
y = \frac{-1 \pm 19}{2}
\]

This gives two solutions for \( y \):
\[
y = \frac{-1 + 19}{2} = 9 \\
y = \frac{-1 - 19}{2} = -10
\]
Since \( y = r^2 \) and squares are non-negative, we discard the negative solution:
\[ y = 9 \]

Now, solve for \( r \):
\[
r^{2} = 9 \\
r = \sqrt{9} \\
r = 3 \quad \text{or} \quad r = -3
\]

Both values are valid in a GP context.

**Final Answer:**
The common ratio of the Geometric Progression is either \(3\) or \(-3\).

```json
{"Final Answer": "3 or -3"}
```

To determine the number of bacteria after 10 hours, I recognize that this is an example of exponential growth. 

The initial count of bacteria is given as 50, and they triple every hour. 

The general formula for exponential growth is:
N = N₀ * (growth factor) ^ time

Here, N₀ represents the initial quantity, which is 50. The growth factor is 3 because the bacteria triple each hour, and the time elapsed is 10 hours.

By substituting these values into the formula, I can calculate the number of bacteria at the end of the 10th hour.
</think>

To determine the number of bacteria present after 10 hours when they triple every hour, we'll use the concept of exponential growth.

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the Initial Quantity (N₀):**
   - The initial number of bacteria is \( N_0 = 50 \).

2. **Determine the Growth Factor and Time (t):**
   - The bacteria triple every hour, so the growth factor (\( r \)) is 3.
   - The time elapsed (\( t \)) is 10 hours.

3. **Apply the Exponential Growth Formula:**
   \[
   N = N_0 \times r^t
   \]
   
4. **Substitute the Known Values into the Formula:**
   \[
   N = 50 \times 3^{10}
   \]

5. **Calculate \( 3^{10} \):**
   - \( 3^{10} = 59,\!049 \)

6. **Multiply to Find the Final Number of Bacteria:**
   \[
   N = 50 \times 59,\!049 = 2,\!952,\!450
   \]

**Final Answer:**

To find the first term (a) and common ratio (r) of the geometric progression, I'll start by using the given information.

First, the sum of the first two terms is -2. In a geometric sequence, each term is multiplied by the common ratio to get the next term. So, the second term can be expressed as 'ar'. Therefore, the equation becomes:

a + ar = -2

This simplifies to:
a(1 + r) = -2  ...(1)

Next, it's given that the fifth term is 16 times the third term. The nth term of a geometric progression is given by an = ar^(n-1). Applying this formula:

Fifth term (ar^4) = 16 × Third term (ar^2)
So,
ar^4 = 16ar^2

Dividing both sides by 'a' and 'r^2':
r^2 = 16
Taking the square root of both sides:
r = ±4

Now, I'll consider each value of r.

Case 1: If r = 4

a(1 + 4) = -2
5a = -2
So,
a = -2/5

Case 2: If r = -4
Substitute r into equation (1):
a(1 + (-4)) = -2
a(-3) = -2
So,
a = 2/3

Both values of a satisfy the given conditions, so there are two possible solutions.
</think>

To find the first term \( a \) and common ratio \( r \) of the geometric progression based on the given conditions:

1. **Sum of the First Two Terms:**
   
   The sum of the first two terms is given by:
   \[
   a + ar = -2
   \]
   Factor out \( a \):
   \[
   a(1 + r) = -2 \quad \text{(Equation 1)}
   \]

2. **Fifth Term and Third Term Relationship:**
   
   The fifth term is given by:
   \[
   ar^4
   \]
   The third term is given by:
   \[
   ar^2
   \]
   According to the problem, the fifth term is 16 times the third term:
   \[
   ar^4 = 16ar^2
   \]
   Divide both sides by \( ar^2 \) (assuming \( a \neq 0 \) and \( r \neq 0 \)):
   \[
   r^2 = 16
   \]
   Taking the square root of both sides:
   \[
   r = \pm 4
   \]

3. **Finding the First Term (\( a \)):**
   
   - **Case 1:** \( r = 4 \)
     
     Substitute \( r = 4 \) into Equation 1:
     \[
     a(1 + 4) = -2 \\
     a(5) = -2 \\
     a = -\frac{2}{5}
     \]

     \[
     a(1 - 4) = -2 \\
     a(-3) = -2 \\
     a = \frac{2}{3}
     \]

Both values of \( a \) satisfy the given conditions.

- **Year 3:** The unpaid balance further reduces to Rs 10,000. Interest here is Rs 2,400, resulting in a total payment of Rs 3,400 for Year 3.

To determine the total cost of the tractor, we'll break down the payments made by the farmer and calculate the interest on each installment.

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Initial Payment:**
   - Cash paid = **Rs 12,000**
   - Remaining balance = **Rs 24,000 - Rs 12,000 = Rs 12,000**

2. **Calculating Annual Installments:**
   Each year, the farmer pays Rs 1,000 towards the principal and 24% interest on the unpaid balance.

   | Year | Unpaid Amount (Rs) | Interest @ 24% | Total Payment (Rs) |
   |------|--------------------|-----------------|---------------------|
   | 1    | **12,000**         | **2,880**       | **3,880**           |
   | 2    | **11,000**         | **2,640**       | **3,640**           |
   | 3    | **10,000**         | **2,400**       | **3,400**           |

3. **Total Installments Paid:**
   - Year 1: Rs 3,880
   - Year 2: Rs 3,640
   - Year 3: Rs 3,400
   - **Total Installments = Rs 3,880 + Rs 3,640 + Rs 3,400 = Rs 10,920**

4. **Grand Total Cost of the Tractor:**
   - Cash Down Payment: Rs 12,000
   - Total Installments: Rs 10,920
   - **Total Cost = Rs 12,000 + Rs 10,920 = Rs 22,920**

Suppose he pays an annual amount of 10% on the outstanding balance plus Rs 2k. After paying for 10 years, we need to determine the remaining balance.

Each year, he pays:
- An interest amount equal to 10% of the outstanding balance.
- A principal reduction of Rs 2k.

This means that each year, the outstanding balance decreases by Rs 2k. Starting from an initial balance of Rs 36,000, we can calculate the remaining balance after 10 years as follows:

\[
\text{Remaining Balance} = \text{Initial Balance} - (\text{Principal Reduction per Year} \times \text{Number of Years})
\]

Substituting the given values:

\[
\text{Remaining Balance} = 36,000 - (2,000 \times 10) = 36,000 - 20,000 = 16,000
\]

Thus, the remaining balance after 10 years will be \boxed{16000}.

I'll start by calculating the simple interest for one year. Simple Interest (SI) can be calculated using the formula SI = P * R * T, where P is the principal amount, R is the rate of interest per annum, and T is the time in years.

For each subsequent year, since it's simple interest, the same calculation applies because the principal remains unchanged after each year. This means that the interest for each year will be identical.

By examining these annual interests, I can confirm that they are constant, which aligns with the definition of an arithmetic progression where the difference between consecutive terms is constant (zero in this case).

Finally, to find the total interest over 30 years, I'll sum up all the individual yearly interests. Since it's an AP with a common difference of zero, the total can be calculated by multiplying the annual interest by the number of years.
</think>

To determine if the interests form an Arithmetic Progression (AP) and calculate the interest at the end of 30 years:

1. **Calculate Annual Interest:**
   
   - Principal (\(P\)) = Rs. 2000
   - Rate (\(R\)) = 16% per annum

   Using the simple interest formula:
   \[
   \text{Simple Interest (SI)} = P \times R \times T
   \]
   
   For one year:
   \[
   SI_{\text{annual}} = 2000 \times 0.16 \times 1 = Rs.\ 320
   \]

2. **Determine if the Series is an AP:**
   
   Since the annual interest remains constant at Rs. 320, each term differs by zero:
   \[
   320,\ 320,\ 320,\ \dots
   \]
   
   This constitutes an Arithmetic Progression with a common difference (\(d\)) of 0.

3. **Calculate Total Interest Over 30 Years:**
   
   The total interest is simply the sum of all annual interests:
   \[
   \text{Total Interest} = SI_{\text{annual}} \times n
   \]
   where \(n = 30\) years.
   
   \[
   \text{Total Interest} = 320 \times 30 = Rs.\ 9600
   \]

### Step-by-Step Solution:

     - First term (\(a_1\)) = 46
     - Common difference (\(d\)) = -2

2. **Understand the Given Information:**
   - Last term (\(a_n\)) = 4

3. **Use the Formula for the nth Term of an Arithmetic Sequence:**
   \[
   a_n = a_1 + (n - 1)d
   \]
   
4. **Plug in the Known Values and Solve for \(n\):**
   \[
   4 = 46 + (n - 1)(-2)
   \]

First, I recognize that we're dealing with an arithmetic progression (AP) which has a total of 100 terms.

I know that the nth term of an AP can be expressed as:
aₙ = a₁ + (n - 1)d

Given the sixth term is 24 and the last term (which is the 100th term) is 212, I'll set up two equations based on these pieces of information.

First equation from the sixth term:
a₁ + 5d = 24

Second equation from the 100th term:
a₁ + 99d = 212

Next, I'll subtract the first equation from the second to eliminate a₁ and solve for d:
(a₁ + 99d) - (a₁ + 5d) = 212 - 24
This simplifies to:
94d = 188

Solving for d gives:
d = 2

Now that I have the common difference, I'll substitute it back into the first equation to find a₁:
a₁ + 5*2 = 24
a₁ = 14

Finally, with both a₁ and d known, I can find the 58th term using the nth term formula:
a₅₈ = 14 + (58 - 1)*2
= 14 + 57*2
= 14 + 114

To solve the problem of finding the **58th** term in the given arithmetic progression (AP), let's follow these steps:

### Given:
- Total number of terms (\(n\)) = 100
- 6th term (\(a_6\)) = 24
- Last term (\(a_{100}\)) = 212

### Step 1: Understanding the Arithmetic Progression (AP)
In an AP, each term is obtained by adding a constant difference (\(d\)) to the previous term. The general formula for the \(n^{th}\) term of an AP is:

\[
a_n = a_1 + (n - 1)d
\]

Where:
- \(a_n\) is the \(n^{th}\) term,
- \(a_1\) is the first term,
- \(d\) is the common difference.

### Step 2: Setting Up Equations Based on Given Information
We are given two specific terms of the AP:

1. The 6th term (\(a_6\)) = 24:
   \[
   a_1 + (6 - 1)d = 24 \\
   a_1 + 5d = 24 \quad \text{(Equation 1)}
   \]

2. The last term (\(a_{100}\)) = 212:
   \[
   a_1 + (100 - 1)d = 212 \\
   a_1 + 99d = 212 \quad \text{(Equation 2)}
   \]

### Step 3: Solving the System of Equations
Subtract **Equation 1** from **Equation 2** to eliminate \(a_1\):

\[
(a_1 + 99d) - (a_1 + 5d) = 212 - 24 \\
94d = 188 \\
d = \frac{188}{94} \\
d = 2
\]

Now, substitute \(d = 2\) back into **Equation 1** to find \(a_1\):

\[
a_1 + 5(2) = 24 \\
a_1 + 10 = 24 \\
a_1 = 24 - 10 \\
a_1 = 14
\]

### Step 4: Finding the 58th Term (\(a_{58}\))
Now, use the general formula for the \(n^{th}\) term to find \(a_{58}\):

\[
a_{58} = a_1 + (58 - 1)d \\
a_{58} = 14 + 57 \times 2 \\
a_{58} = 14 + 114 \\
a_{58} = 128
\]

### Final Answer:
The **58th** term of the arithmetic progression is:

```json
{"Final Answer": "128"}
```

\[

\[

\]

To determine the year when Subba Rao's income reaches ₹14,000, we can follow these steps:

**Given:**
- Starting Year = 1995
- Initial Annual Salary (a) = ₹10,000
- Annual Increment (d) = ₹400
- Target Salary = ₹14,000

**Solution:**

We can use the formula for an arithmetic sequence to find out after how many years his salary reaches ₹14,000.

The general formula for the \( n^{th} \) term of an arithmetic sequence is:
\[
a_n = a + (n - 1)d
\]
where:
- \( a_n \) is the \( n^{th} \) term,
- \( a \) is the first term,
- \( d \) is the common difference,
- \( n \) is the number of terms.

We need to find \( n \) when \( a_n = ₹14,000 \).

\[
14,000 = 10,000 + (n - 1) \times 400
\]

**Step 1:** Subtract 10,000 from both sides:
\[
14,000 - 10,000 = (n - 1) \times 400 \\
4,000 = (n - 1) \times 400
\]

**Step 2:** Divide both sides by 400 to solve for \( n \):
\[
\frac{4,000}{400} = n - 1 \\
10 = n - 1
\]

**Step 3:** Add 1 to both sides:
\[
n = 10 + 1 \\
n = 11
\]

This means that in the \( 11^{th} \) year after starting work, his salary will reach ₹14,000.

**Calculating the Year:**
- Starting Year = 1995
- After 10 years (since the first term is at n=1): 
\[
1995 + 10 = 2005
\]

So, **in the year 2005**, Subba Rao's income reaches ₹14,000.

{Final Answer: "2005"}

To find the sum of the first 44 terms of an arithmetic progression (AP) where the common difference (\(d\)) is 14 and the 44th term (\(a_{44}\)) is 298, we can follow these steps:

### Step 1: Find the First Term (\(a_1\))

The general formula for the \(n^{th}\) term of an AP is:
\[ a_n = a_1 + (n - 1)d \]

Given:
- \(d = 14\)
- \(a_{44} = 298\)

Substitute these values into the formula to find \(a_1\):
\[
\begin{align*}
a_{44} &= a_1 + (44 - 1) \times 14 \\
298 &= a_1 + 43 \times 14 \\
298 &= a_1 + 602 \\
a_1 &= 298 - 602 \\
a_1 &= -304
\end{align*}
\]

### Step 2: Calculate the Sum of the First 44 Terms (\(S_{44}\))

The formula for the sum of the first \(n\) terms of an AP is:
\[ S_n = \frac{n}{2} (a_1 + a_n) \]

Substitute the known values into the formula:
\[
\begin{align*}
S_{44} &= \frac{44}{2} \times (-304 + 298) \\
&= 22 \times (-6) \\
&= -132
\end{align*}
\]

### Final Answer

The sum of the first 44 terms is:
\[ \boxed{-132} \]

\[
\frac{a + b}{2} = 10
\]
Multiplying both sides by 2 gives:
\[
a + b = 20
\]

\[
\sqrt{ab} = 8
\]

\[

\]

Now, I have a system of equations:
1. \( a + b = 20 \)
2. \( ab = 64 \)

To solve this system, I can express one variable in terms of the other using the first equation and substitute it into the second equation.

Let's express \( a \) as:
\[
a = 20 - b
\]

Substituting this into the second equation:
\[
(20 - b)b = 64
\]
Expanding gives:
\[
20b - b^2 = 64
\]
Rearranging terms to form a quadratic equation:
\[
b^2 - 20b + 64 = 0
\]

\[

\]

\[
b = \frac{20 \pm 12}{2}
\]

1. \( b = \frac{32}{2} = 16 \)
2. \( b = \frac{8}{2} = 4 \)

Correspondingly, the values of \( a \) are:

2. If \( b = 4 \), then \( a = 20 - 4 = 16 \)

To find the two positive numbers \( a \) and \( b \) given their Arithmetic Mean (AM) and Geometric Mean (GM), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Understanding AM and GM
- **Arithmetic Mean (AM)** of two numbers \( a \) and \( b \) is calculated as:
  \[
  \text{AM} = \frac{a + b}{2}
  \]
  Given that the AM is 10:
  \[
  \frac{a + b}{2} = 10
  \]
  
- **Geometric Mean (GM)** of two numbers \( a \) and \( b \) is calculated as:
  \[
  \text{GM} = \sqrt{ab}
  \]
  Given that the GM is 8:
  \[
  \sqrt{ab} = 8
  \]

### Step 2: Setting Up Equations
From the given AM and GM, we have two equations:
1. \( a + b = 20 \) (Multiplying both sides of the AM equation by 2)
2. \( ab = 64 \) (Squaring both sides of the GM equation)

### Step 3: Solving the System of Equations
We can solve this system using substitution or other methods. Here, we'll use substitution:

1. From the first equation:
   \[
   a = 20 - b
   \]
   
2. Substitute \( a \) into the second equation:
   \[
   (20 - b) \times b = 64
   \]
   Simplifying:
   \[
   20b - b^2 = 64
   \]
   
3. Rearranging terms to form a quadratic equation:
   \[
   b^2 - 20b + 64 = 0
   \]
   
4. Solving the quadratic equation using the quadratic formula \( b = \frac{-B \pm \sqrt{B^2 - 4AC}}{2A} \), where \( A = 1 \), \( B = -20 \), and \( C = 64 \):
   \[
   b = \frac{20 \pm \sqrt{(20)^2 - 4(1)(64)}}{2}
   \]
   Calculating further:
   \[
   b = \frac{20 \pm \sqrt{400 - 256}}{2} = \frac{20 \pm \sqrt{144}}{2} = \frac{20 \pm 12}{2}
   \]
   
5. This yields two solutions:
   \[
   b = \frac{32}{2} = 16 \quad \text{and} \quad b = \frac{8}{2} = 4
   \]

6. Correspondingly, the values of \( a \) are:
   - If \( b = 16 \), then \( a = 20 - 16 = 4 \)
   - If \( b = 4 \), then \( a = 20 - 4 = 16 \)

### Step 4: Final Answer
The two positive numbers are **16** and **4**.

Similarly, the GM is given as 6, so:

√(αβ) = 6  
Squaring both sides:  
αβ = 36

Now, for a quadratic equation with roots α and β, the standard form is x² - (sum of roots)x + product of roots = 0. So substituting the values we have:

Sum of roots = α + β = 32  
Product of roots = αβ = 36  

Therefore, the quadratic equation should be:  
x² - 32x + 36 = 0

GM = 6 implies √(αβ) = 6 => αβ = 36  

So the quadratic equation is x² - (sum)x + product = 0, which would be x² - 32x + 36 = 0.

But just to make sure I didn't mix up any steps, let me verify:

If a quadratic equation has roots α and β, then it can be written as (x - α)(x - β) = 0  
Expanding this: x² - (α + β)x + αβ = 0

So yes, that matches what I did earlier. Therefore, the correct quadratic equation is x² -32x +36=0.

The quadratic equation with roots having an Arithmetic Mean of 16 and a Geometric Mean of 6 can be found by using the sum and product of the roots.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. Let α and β be the roots of the quadratic equation.
2. The Arithmetic Mean (AM) of α and β is given by (α + β)/2 = 16 ⇒ α + β = 32.
3. The Geometric Mean (GM) of α and β is given by √(αβ) = 6 ⇒ αβ = 36.
4. A standard quadratic equation with roots α and β is x² - (α + β)x + αβ = 0.
5. Substituting the values from steps 2 and 3, we get:
   x² - 32x + 36 = 0.

**Final Answer:**

The quadratic equation is \boxed{x^2 - 32x + 36 = 0}.

To calculate the geometric mean of the annual percentage growth rate of profits from 2000 to 2005, I first need to determine the total number of periods. Since we're looking at data from 2000 to 2005 inclusive, that's six years.

</think>

To calculate the **geometric mean** of the annual percentage growth rates of profits from 2000 to 2005, follow these steps:

### Given Data:
The annual profit growth rates are:  
50, 72, 54, 82, 93  

### Step 1: Determine the Number of Periods
- **Number of years (n)** = 6

### Step 2: Calculate the Product of All Growth Rates
\[
\text{Product} = 50 \times 72 \times 54 \times 82 \times 93
\]
\[
\text{Product} = 164,\!450,\!640
\]

### Step 3: Calculate the Geometric Mean
The geometric mean (\(GM\)) is calculated using the formula:
\[
GM = \left( \prod_{i=1}^{n} x_i \right)^{\frac{1}{n}}
\]
Where:
- \(x_i\) are the growth rates (50, 72, 54, 82, 93)
- \(n = 6\)

Plugging in the values:
\[
GM = \left( 164,\!450,\!640 \right)^{\frac{1}{6}}
\]
\[
GM \approx 7.41
\]

### Final Answer:
The geometric mean of the annual percentage growth rate is:

\[
\boxed{7.41}
\]

To determine the value of \( k \) such that the arithmetic mean of the numbers 9, 8, 10, \( k \), and 12 is 15, follow these steps:

### Step 1: Understand the Arithmetic Mean Formula
The arithmetic mean (\( \mu \)) of a set of numbers is given by:
\[
\mu = \frac{\text{Sum of all values}}{\text{Number of values}}
\]

### Step 2: Set Up the Equation
Given that the mean is 15 and there are 5 numbers in total, we can write:
\[
15 = \frac{9 + 8 + 10 + k + 12}{5}
\]

### Step 3: Simplify the Numerator
First, add the known values:
\[
9 + 8 + 10 + 12 = 39
\]
So, the equation becomes:
\[
15 = \frac{39 + k}{5}
\]

### Step 4: Solve for \( k \)
Multiply both sides of the equation by 5 to eliminate the denominator:
\[
15 \times 5 = 39 + k
\]
\[
75 = 39 + k
\]

Subtract 39 from both sides to isolate \( k \):
\[
k = 75 - 39
\]
\[
k = 36
\]

### Final Answer
The value of \( k \) is:
\[
\boxed{36}
\]

**Solution:**

We are given that the mean of 14 numbers is 6. To find the new mean when 3 is added to every number, follow these steps:

1. **Find the Original Total Sum:**
   
   The mean (\( \bar{x} \)) is calculated as:
   \[
   \bar{x} = \frac{\text{Sum of numbers}}{\text{Number of terms}}
   \]
   
   Given \( \bar{x} = 6 \) and number of terms (\( n \)) = 14, the total sum (\( S \)) is:
   \[
   S = \bar{x} \times n = 6 \times 14 = 84
   \]

2. **Calculate the New Sum After Adding 3 to Each Number:**
   
   If we add 3 to each of the 14 numbers, the total increase is:
   \[
   \text{Total Increase} = 3 \times 14 = 42
   \]
   
   Therefore, the new sum (\( S' \)) becomes:
   \[
   S' = S + \text{Total Increase} = 84 + 42 = 126
   \]

3. **Determine the New Mean:**
   
   The new mean (\( \bar{x'} \)) is:
   \[
   \bar{x'} = \frac{S'}{n} = \frac{126}{14} = 9
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{9}

- The second term \( a_2 = 10 \)

\[ d = a_2 - a_1 = 10 - 5 = 5 \]

Thus, the first five terms of the sequence are:
\[ 5, 10, 15, 20, 25 \]

To find the sum of these five terms (the corresponding series), we use the formula for the sum of an arithmetic series:

To find the **12th term** of the given Geometric Progression (GP), let's follow these steps:

### Given:
- **First term (\( a_1 \))**: 7
- **Common ratio (\( r \))**: Calculated by dividing the second term by the first term.
  
  \[
  r = \frac{49}{7} = 7
  \]

### Formula for the \( n \)-th Term of a GP:
\[
a_n = a_1 \times r^{(n-1)}
\]

### Calculation for the 12th Term (\( a_{12} \)):
\[
a_{12} = 7 \times 7^{(12-1)} = 7 \times 7^{11} = 7^{12}
\]

Therefore, the **12th term** of the Geometric Progression is:

\[
\boxed{7^{12}}
\]

First, I identify that the given sequence \(4, 64, 1024, \ldots\) forms a geometric progression. In a geometric sequence, each term after the first is obtained by multiplying the previous term by a constant called the common ratio (\(r\)).

Next, I calculate the common ratio by dividing the second term by the first term:
\[ r = \frac{64}{4} = 16 \]

With the common ratio known, I use the general formula for the \(n^{th}\) term of a geometric progression:
\[ a_n = a_1 \times r^{(n-1)} \]
where \(a_1\) is the first term.

I set up the equation using the given value \(262144\):
\[ 262144 = 4 \times 16^{(n-1)} \]

To solve for \(n\), I divide both sides by 4:
\[ 65536 = 16^{(n-1)} \]

Recognizing that \(65536\) is a power of 16, specifically \(16^4 = 65536\), I equate the exponents:
\[ n - 1 = 4 \]
\[ n = 5 \]

Thus, the term \(262144\) corresponds to the 5th position in the geometric progression.

To determine which term of the geometric progression (GP) \(4, 64, 1024, \ldots\) is \(262144\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Identify the GP Parameters
- **First term (\(a_1\))**: 4
- **Common ratio (\(r\))**:
  
  The common ratio of a GP can be found by dividing the second term by the first term.
  
  \[
  r = \frac{64}{4} = 16
  \]

### Step 2: Use the General Formula for the \(n^{th}\) Term of a GP

The general formula for the \(n^{th}\) term (\(a_n\)) of a geometric progression is:
  
  \[
  a_n = a_1 \times r^{(n-1)}
  \]
  
### Step 3: Set Up the Equation to Find the Term Equal to 262144

We need to find \(n\) such that:

\[
262144 = 4 \times 16^{(n-1)}
\]

**Solution:**

1. **Divide both sides by 4** to isolate the exponential term:
   
   \[
   \frac{262144}{4} = 16^{(n-1)} \\
   65536 = 16^{(n-1)}
   \]

2. **Express 65536 as a Power of 16**:
   
   Notice that \(16^4 = 65536\). Therefore,
   
   \[
   16^{(n-1)} = 16^4
   \]
   
3. **Set the Exponents Equal to Each Other** (since the bases are equal):
   
   \[
   n - 1 = 4
   \]
   
4. **Solve for \(n\)**:
   
   \[
   n = 4 + 1 \\
   n = 5
   \]

### Final Answer

The term \(262144\) is the \(5^{th}\) term of the geometric progression.

To find the sum \( S_n \) of the series up to \( n \) terms, we start by recognizing that each term in the series is a geometric sequence. The general form of the series is:

\[ 9 + 6 + 4 + \ldots \]

This can be written as:

\[ 9 \left(1 + \frac{2}{3} + \left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^2 + \ldots + \left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^{n-1}\right) \]

The series inside the parentheses is a geometric series with the first term \( a = 1 \) and common ratio \( r = \frac{2}{3} \). The sum of the first \( n \) terms of a geometric series is given by:

\[ S_n = a \frac{1 - r^n}{1 - r} \]

Substituting \( a = 1 \) and \( r = \frac{2}{3} \):

\[ S_n = \frac{1 - \left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^n}{1 - \frac{2}{3}} \]

Simplifying the denominator:

\[ 1 - \frac{2}{3} = \frac{1}{3} \]

Thus, the sum becomes:

\[ S_n = 9 \times \frac{1 - \left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^n}{\frac{1}{3}} = 9 \times 3 \left(1 - \left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^n\right) = 9 \left(1 - \left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^n\right) \]

Therefore, the sum \( S_n \) of the series up to \( n \) terms is:

\[ \boxed{9\left(1 - \left(\dfrac{2}{3}\right)^n\right)} \]

</think>

To determine the number of terms required from the geometric progression (GP) \(3, \frac{3}{2}, \frac{3}{4}, \ldots\) to achieve a sum of \(\frac{6141}{1024}\), we use the formula for the sum of the first \(n\) terms of a GP:

\[
S_n = a \cdot \frac{1 - r^n}{1 - r}
\]

where:
- \(a = 3\) (the first term),
- \(r = \frac{1}{2}\) (the common ratio),
- \(S_n = \frac{6141}{1024}\).

Substituting the known values into the formula:

\[
\frac{6141}{1024} = 3 \cdot \frac{1 - \left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^n}{1 - \frac{1}{2}}
\]

Simplifying the denominator \(1 - \frac{1}{2} = \frac{1}{2}\), so:

\[
\frac{6141}{1024} = 3 \cdot \left(1 - \left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^n\right) \cdot 2
\]

This simplifies to:

\[
\frac{6141}{1024} = 6 \cdot \left(1 - \left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^n\right)
\]

Dividing both sides by 6:

\[
\frac{6141}{6144} = 1 - \left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^n
\]

Rearranging gives:

\[
\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^n = 1 - \frac{6141}{6144} = \frac{3}{6144} = \frac{1}{2048}
\]

Recognizing that \(\frac{1}{2048} = \left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{11}\), we find:

\[
n = 11
\]

**Answer:** {Final Answer: 11}

**Solution:**

To find the 14th term of a geometric progression (GP) where the common ratio \( r = 4 \) and the 10th term is given as 384, we can use the formula for the \( n \)-th term of a GP:

\[
a_n = a_1 \times r^{(n-1)}
\]

Where:
- \( a_n \) is the \( n \)-th term
- \( a_1 \) is the first term
- \( r \) is the common ratio
- \( n \) is the term number

**Step 1:** Find the First Term (\( a_1 \))

Given:
- The 10th term, \( a_{10} = 384 \)
- Common ratio, \( r = 4 \)

Using the formula for the 10th term:

\[
a_{10} = a_1 \times r^{(10-1)} \\
384 = a_1 \times 4^{9}
\]

Calculate \( 4^9 \):

\[
4^9 = 262,144
\]

So,

\[
a_1 = \frac{384}{262,144} \\
a_1 = 0.00146484375
\]

**Step 2:** Find the 14th Term (\( a_{14} \))

Now, use \( a_1 \) to find \( a_{14} \):

\[
a_{14} = a_1 \times r^{(14-1)} \\
a_{14} = 0.00146484375 \times 4^{13}
\]

Calculate \( 4^{13} \):

\[
4^{13} = 2,097,152
\]

Therefore,

\[
a_{14} = 0.00146484375 \times 2,097,152 \\
a_{14} = 2,097,152
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{2,\!097,\!152}
\]

To determine the **9th** term of the geometric progression (GP) where the 6th term is equal to the square of the 4th term and the first term (\(a_1\)) is \(-10\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Understand the GP Formula
In a GP, each term can be expressed as:
\[ a_n = a \cdot r^{n-1} \]
where:
- \(a\) is the first term,
- \(r\) is the common ratio,
- \(n\) is the term number.

### Step 2: Set Up Equations Based on Given Information
Given:
- \(a_6 = (a_4)^2\)
- First term, \(a = -10\)

Using the GP formula for the 4th and 6th terms:
\[ a_4 = a \cdot r^{3} \]
\[ a_6 = a \cdot r^{5} \]

Substitute these into the given equation:
\[ a \cdot r^{5} = (a \cdot r^{3})^2 \]
\[ -10 \cdot r^{5} = (-10)^{2} \cdot r^{6} \]
\[ -10r^{5} = 100r^{6} \]

### Step 3: Solve for \(r\)
Divide both sides by \(r^{5}\) (assuming \(r \neq 0\)):
\[ -10 = 100r \]
\[ r = \frac{-10}{100} \]
\[ r = -0.1 \]

### Step 4: Find the 9th Term (\(a_9\))

\[ a_9 = a \cdot r^{8} \]
\[ a_9 = -10 \cdot (-0.1)^{8} \]

Calculate \((-0.1)^8\):
Since any even power of a negative number is positive,
\[ (-0.1)^8 = (0.1)^8 = 1 \times 10^{-8} = 0.00000001 \]

Thus:
\[ a_9 = -10 \cdot 0.00000001 \]
\[ a_9 = -0.0000001 \]

### Final Answer
The **9th** term of the geometric progression is:

\[
\boxed{-0.0000001}

Given the problem, we need to find the common ratio of a geometric progression where the third term is 10r² and the fifth term is 10r⁴. The sum of these terms is given as 900.

1. The first term is 10.
2. The third term is \(10r^2\).
3. The fifth term is \(10r^4\).
4. The sum of the third and fifth terms is \(10r^2 + 10r^4 = 900\).

Dividing both sides by 10, we get:
\[ r^2 + r^4 = 90 \]

Let \( x = r^2 \). Then the equation becomes:
\[ x + x^2 = 90 \]
\[ x^2 + x - 90 = 0 \]

Solving this quadratic equation using the quadratic formula:
\[ x = \frac{-1 \pm \sqrt{1 + 4 \cdot 90}}{2} = \frac{-1 \pm 19}{2} \]

The positive solution is:
\[ x = \frac{18}{2} = 9 \]

Thus, \( r^2 = 9 \), which gives \( r = 3 \) or \( r = -3 \).

Therefore, the common ratio can be either 3 or -3.

The problem involves finding the first term of a geometric progression where the sum of the first two terms is -2, and the fifth term is 16 times the third term. Through solving the equations derived from these conditions, we find two possible values for the first term: \(-\frac{2}{5}\) and \(\frac{2}{3}\).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Define Variables:**
   - Let \( a \) be the first term.
   - Let \( r \) be the common ratio.

2. **Set Up Equations Based on Given Conditions:**
   - Sum of the first two terms:
     \[
     a + ar = -2
     \]
     This simplifies to:
     \[
     a(1 + r) = -2 \quad (1)
     \]
   - Fifth term is 16 times the third term:
     \[
     ar^4 = 16 \cdot ar^2
     \]
     Simplifying this gives:
     \[
     r^2 = 16 \quad (2)
     \]

3. **Solve for \( r \):**
   From equation (2):
   \[
   r^2 = 16 \implies r = \pm4
   \]
   
4. **Find \( a \) for Each Value of \( r \):**
   - For \( r = 4 \):
     Substitute into equation (1):
     \[
     a(1 + 4) = -2 \implies 5a = -2 \implies a = -\frac{2}{5}
     \]
   - For \( r = -4 \):
     Substitute into equation (1):
     \[
     a(1 - 4) = -2 \implies -3a = -2 \implies a = \frac{2}{3}
     \]

5. **Verification:**
   Both values of \( a \) satisfy the original conditions, so they are valid solutions.

**Final Answer:**  
There are two possible first terms: \(-\frac{2}{5}\) and \(\frac{2}{3}\).

To determine if 330 is a term in the sequence 5, 11, 17, 23, ..., I'll start by identifying the pattern.

First, I observe that each number increases by 6 from the previous one. This indicates that the sequence is arithmetic with a common difference of 6.

The general formula for the nth term of an arithmetic sequence is:
\[ a_n = a_1 + (n - 1)d \]
where \(a_1\) is the first term and \(d\) is the common difference.

Plugging in the known values:
- First term (\(a_1\)) = 5
- Common difference (\(d\)) = 6

The formula becomes:
\[ a_n = 5 + (n - 1) \times 6 \]
Simplifying this gives:
\[ a_n = 6n - 1 \]

Now, I'll set \(a_n\) equal to 330 and solve for \(n\):
\[ 6n - 1 = 330 \]
Adding 1 to both sides:
\[ 6n = 331 \]
Dividing both sides by 6:
\[ n = \frac{331}{6} \approx 55.17 \]

Since \(n\) must be an integer for it to be a term in the sequence, and 55.17 is not an integer, 330 is not part of the given arithmetic progression.

To determine whether **330** is a term of the arithmetic sequence \(5, 11, 17, 23, \ldots\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Identify the Sequence Parameters
- **First term (\(a_1\))**: 5
- **Common difference (\(d\))**: Each term increases by 6 (e.g., \(11 - 5 = 6\))

### Step 2: Use the General Formula for an Arithmetic Sequence
The nth term of an arithmetic sequence can be found using:
\[ a_n = a_1 + (n - 1)d \]

Plugging in the known values:
\[ a_n = 5 + (n - 1) \times 6 \]
Simplify the equation:
\[ a_n = 6n - 1 \]

### Step 3: Set Up the Equation to Find \(n\) When \(a_n = 330\)

**Step 4: Solve for \(n\)**
Add 1 to both sides:
\[ 6n = 331 \]
Divide both sides by 6:
\[ n = \frac{331}{6} \approx 55.17 \]

### Step 5: Interpret the Result

To determine if the interests form an arithmetic progression (AP), I'll first calculate the simple interest for each year.

For a principal amount \( P = 2000 \) at a rate \( R = 16\% \), the simple interest formula is:
\[ \text{Interest} = \frac{P \times R \times T}{100} \]

In this case, since the time period \( T \) increases by one year each time, I can observe how the interest changes annually.

For Year 1:
\[ \text{Interest}_1 = \frac{2000 \times 16 \times 1}{100} = 320 \]

For Year 2:
\[ \text{Interest}_2 = \frac{2000 \times 16 \times 2}{100} = 640 \]

Continuing this pattern, each subsequent year's interest increases by \( 320 \), which means the interests form an AP with a common difference of \( 320 \).

To find the total interest over 30 years, I'll use the sum formula for an AP:
\[ S_n = \frac{n}{2} \times [2a + (n - 1)d] \]
Where:
- \( n = 30 \)
- \( a = 320 \) (first term)
- \( d = 320 \) (common difference)

Plugging in the values:
\[ S_{30} = \frac{30}{2} \times [640 + (29)(320)] \]
\[ S_{30} = 15 \times (640 + 9280) \]
\[ S_{30} = 15 \times 9920 \]
\[ S_{30} = 148800 \]

Therefore, the total interest earned over 30 years is \( 148,\!800 \).

### Step 1: Understanding Simple Interest
The formula for simple interest is:
\[
\text{Interest} = \frac{P \times R \times T}{100}
\]
where:
- \( P = 2000 \) (Principal amount)
- \( R = 16\% \) (Rate of interest per annum)
- \( T \) is the time in years.

### Step 2: Calculating Annual Interests
For each year, substitute \( T = 1, 2, 3, \ldots \) into the formula:
\[
\text{Interest}_n = \frac{2000 \times 16 \times n}{100} = 320n
\]

This shows that each year's interest increases linearly by \( 320 \), indicating an **Arithmetic Progression (AP)** with:
- First term (\( a \)) = 320
- Common difference (\( d \)) = 320

### Step 3: Sum of Interests Over 30 Years
The sum of the first \( n \) terms of an AP is given by:
\[
S_n = \frac{n}{2} \times [2a + (n - 1)d]
\]

For \( n = 30 \):

S_{30} = \frac{30}{2} \times [2(320) + (30 - 1)(320)]
\]
\[
S_{30} = 15 \times [640 + 9280]
\]
\[
S_{30} = 15 \times 9920
\]
\[
S_{30} = 148,800
\]

The interests form an AP. The total interest at the end of 30 years is:

***********************<think>
First, I identify that this problem involves an Arithmetic Progression (AP). In an AP, each term increases by a constant difference \( d \).

Given:
- The 6th term (\( T_6 \)) is 24.
- The last term (\( T_{100} \)) is 212.

I'll use the formula for the nth term of an AP: 
\[
T_n = a + (n - 1)d
\]
where \( a \) is the first term and \( d \) is the common difference.

From \( T_6 = 24 \), I can write:
\[
a + 5d = 24 \quad (1)
\]

And from \( T_{100} = 212 \), I have:
\[
a + 99d = 212 \quad (2)

Subtracting equation (1) from equation (2):
\[
(a + 99d) - (a + 5d) = 212 - 24
\]
\[
94d = 188
\]
\[
d = 2

Now that I have \( d \), I can substitute back into equation (1):
\[
a + 5(2) = 24
\]
\[
a + 10 = 24
\]
\[
a = 14

Finally, to find the 58th term (\( T_{58} \)):
\[
T_{58} = a + 57d
\]
\[
= 14 + 57 \times 2
\]
\[
= 14 + 114
\]
\[
= 128

So, the 58th term of the arithmetic progression is 128.
</think>

Let's solve the problem step by step.

**Given:**
- The \(6^{\text{th}}\) term (\(T_6\)) = 24
- The last (i.e., \(100^{\text{th}}\)) term (\(T_{100}\)) = 212

We need to find the \(58^{\text{th}}\) term (\(T_{58}\)) of this arithmetic progression.

**Step 1: Understanding the Arithmetic Progression (AP)**
In an AP, each term after the first is obtained by adding a constant difference (\(d\)) to the preceding term. The general formula for the \(n^{\text{th}}\) term of an AP is:
\[
T_n = a + (n - 1)d
\]
where:
- \(a\) is the first term,
- \(d\) is the common difference,
- \(n\) is the number of terms.

**Step 2: Set Up Equations Using Given Terms**
Using the given terms, we can set up two equations:

1. For the \(6^{\text{th}}\) term (\(T_6 = 24\)):
\[
a + (6 - 1)d = 24 \quad \Rightarrow \quad a + 5d = 24 \quad (1)
\]

2. For the \(100^{\text{th}}\) term (\(T_{100} = 212\)):
\[
a + (100 - 1)d = 212 \quad \Rightarrow \quad a + 99d = 212 \quad (2)
\]

**Step 3: Solve for Common Difference (\(d\))**
Subtract equation (1) from equation (2) to eliminate \(a\):
\[
(a + 99d) - (a + 5d) = 212 - 24
\]
\[
94d = 188 \quad \Rightarrow \quad d = \frac{188}{94} = 2
\]

**Step 4: Solve for First Term (\(a\))**
Substitute \(d = 2\) back into equation (1):
\[
a + 5(2) = 24 \quad \Rightarrow \quad a + 10 = 24 \quad \Rightarrow \quad a = 14
\]

**Step 5: Find the \(58^{\text{th}}\) Term (\(T_{58}\))**
Now, use the general formula for the \(n^{\text{th}}\) term with \(n = 58\):
\[
T_{58} = a + (58 - 1)d
\]
Substitute \(a = 14\) and \(d = 2\):
\[
T_{58} = 14 + 57 \times 2 = 14 + 114 = 128
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{128}
\]

- **Initial Salary:** ₹10,000 per annum
- **Annual Increment:** ₹400

**Objective:**
Find the year when his salary reaches ₹14,000.

---

### **Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Calculate the Total Increase Needed:**
   
   \[
   \text{Total Increase} = \text{Target Salary} - \text{Initial Salary}
   \]
   
   \[
   \text{Total Increase} = ₹14,000 - ₹10,000 = ₹4,000
   \]

2. **Determine the Number of Years Required:**

   \[
   n = \frac{\text{Total Increase}}{\text{Annual Increment}}
   \]
   
   \[

   \]

   \text{Target Year} = 1995 + 10 = 2005
   \]

I know that in an AP, each term increases by a common difference. So, the difference between consecutive terms should be consistent.

By subtracting the second term from the third term, I can find the common difference:
Common difference (d) = 36 - 28 = 8

With the common difference known, I can determine the first term of the AP. Since the second term is equal to the first term plus the common difference:
First term (a) = Second term - d = 28 - 8 = 20

Now that I have both the first term and the common difference, I can use the formula for the sum of the first n terms of an AP:
Sum (Sₙ) = n/2 × [2a + (n-1)d]

Substituting the known values into the formula to find the sum of the first 102 terms:
S₁₀₂ = (102)/2 × [2(20) + (102 - 1)(8)]
= 51 × [40 + 16×8]
= 51 × [40 + 128]
= 51 × 168
= 8568

Therefore, the sum of the first 102 terms is 8,568.

**Solution:**

We are given an Arithmetic Progression (AP) where:
- The second term (\(a_2\)) = 28
- The third term (\(a_3\)) = 36

**Step 1: Find the Common Difference (\(d\))**

In an AP, each term increases by a common difference \(d\).

\[
a_3 - a_2 = d \Rightarrow 36 - 28 = d \Rightarrow d = 8
\]

**Step 2: Find the First Term (\(a_1\))**

Using the second term:

\[
a_2 = a_1 + d \Rightarrow 28 = a_1 + 8 \Rightarrow a_1 = 20
\]

**Step 3: Calculate the Sum of the First 102 Terms (\(S_{102}\))**

The sum of the first \(n\) terms of an AP is given by:

\[
S_n = \frac{n}{2} \times [2a_1 + (n - 1)d]
\]

Substituting \(n = 102\), \(a_1 = 20\), and \(d = 8\):

\[
S_{102} = \frac{102}{2} \times [2 \times 20 + (102 - 1) \times 8] \\
= 51 \times [40 + 16 \times 8] \\
= 51 \times [40 + 128] \\
= 51 \times 168 \\
= 8568
\]

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{8568}
\]

To determine how many terms of the arithmetic progression (AP) \(24, 21, 18, \ldots\) must be taken so that their sum is **78**, follow these steps:

### Step 1: Identify the First Term and Common Difference
- **First term (\(a\))**: 24
- **Common difference (\(d\))**: \(21 - 24 = -3\)

### Step 2: Use the Sum Formula for an AP
The sum of the first \(n\) terms of an AP is given by:
\[ S_n = \frac{n}{2} \times [2a + (n - 1)d] \]

Given that the sum (\(S_n\)) should be **78**, set up the equation:
\[ 78 = \frac{n}{2} \times [2 \times 24 + (n - 1)(-3)] \]

### Step 3: Simplify the Equation
Substitute the known values into the formula:
\[ 78 = \frac{n}{2} \times [48 - 3(n - 1)] \]
\[ 78 = \frac{n}{2} \times (51 - 3n) \]
Multiply both sides by 2 to eliminate the fraction:
\[ 156 = n(51 - 3n) \]
\[ 156 = 51n - 3n^2 \]

### Step 4: Rearrange into a Quadratic Equation
Bring all terms to one side to form a quadratic equation:
\[ 3n^2 - 51n + 156 = 0 \]

Divide the entire equation by 3 to simplify:
\[ n^2 - 17n + 52 = 0 \]

### Step 5: Solve the Quadratic Equation
Use the quadratic formula \(n = \frac{-b \pm \sqrt{b^2 - 4ac}}{2a}\), where \(a = 1\), \(b = -17\), and \(c = 52\):
\[ n = \frac{17 \pm \sqrt{(-17)^2 - 4(1)(52)}}{2} \]
\[ n = \frac{17 \pm \sqrt{289 - 208}}{2} \]
\[ n = \frac{17 \pm \sqrt{81}}{2} \]
\[ n = \frac{17 \pm 9}{2} \]

This gives two possible solutions:
- \(n = \frac{17 + 9}{2} = 13\)
- \(n = \frac{17 - 9}{2} = 4\)

### Step 6: Verify the Valid Solutions
Since we're dealing with a count of terms, only **positive integers** are valid:
- Both **\(n = 13\)** and **\(n = 4\)** satisfy the equation.

### Final Answer
The number of terms that must be taken so that their sum is 78 can be either **4** or **13**. However, since we're typically looking for the smallest number of terms required to reach a specific sum, the primary solution is:

```json
{"Final Answer": "4"}
```

The arithmetic mean of two positive numbers \(a\) and \(b\) being 10 implies:
\[
\frac{a + b}{2} = 10 \implies a + b = 20
\]

The geometric mean being 8 implies:
\[
\sqrt{ab} = 8 \implies ab = 64
\]

We solve the system of equations:
\[
a + b = 20 \\
ab = 64
\]

Substituting \(b = 20 - a\) into \(ab = 64\):
\[
a(20 - a) = 64 \implies -a^2 + 20a - 64 = 0 \\
a^2 - 20a + 64 = 0
\]

Factoring:
\[
(a - 4)(a - 16) = 0 \implies a = 4 \text{ or } 16
\]

Thus, the numbers are \(4\) and \(16\).

To calculate the geometric mean of the annual percentage growth rates for profits from 2000 to 2005, I first need to determine the individual growth rates for each year.

The formula for calculating the growth rate between two consecutive years is:

Growth Rate = (Next Value / Previous Value - 1) × 100%

Using this formula, I can calculate each year's growth rate based on the given profit figures: 50, 72, 54, 82, and 93.

Once I have all five growth rates, I will compute their geometric mean by multiplying them together and then taking the fifth root of the product. This approach is appropriate for finding an average growth rate over multiple periods because it accounts for compounding effects more accurately than the arithmetic mean.
